Low regularity well-posedness for two-dimensional deep water waves
Lizhe Wan

Abstract
. The study of gravity-capillary water waves in two space dimensions has been an important question in mathematical fluid dynamics. By implementing the cubic modified energy method of Ifrim-Tataru in the context of gravity-capillary waves, we show that for s > 1 s>1 , the two-dimensional gravity-capillary water wave system is locally well-posed in ℋ s \mathcal{H}^{s} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
76B15, 35Q31

1.
Introduction

In this article, we consider the local well-posedness of two-dimensional inviscid incompressible gravity-capillary water waves in infinite depth.
Mathematically, the water waves can be described using the incompressible Euler’s equations with free boundary conditions on the upper surface.

We start by briefly discussing the free boundary Euler equations and the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem formulation for the system.
Let the water domain at time
t
be
\Omega_{t}
, and the boundary at time
t
be
\Gamma_{t}
.
The boundary
\Gamma_{t}
is assumed to be asymptotically flat at infinity or periodic with zero mean.
The fluid velocity is denoted by
\mathbf{u}(t,x,y)
, and the pressure is denoted by
p(t,x,y)
.
Then
(\mathbf{u},p)
solve the following Euler equations inside
\Omega_{t}
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{u}_{t}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{u}=\nabla p-g\mathbf{e}_{2}\\
&\text{div }\mathbf{u}=0\\
&\mathbf{u}(0,x)=\mathbf{u}_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

while on the boundary we have the kinematic boundary condition

\partial_{t}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\text{ is tangent to }\bigcup\Gamma_{t},

and also the dynamic boundary condition

p=-2\sigma\mathbb{H}\ \ \text{ on }\Gamma_{t}.

Here,
\mathbb{H}
is the mean curvature of the boundary,
g\geq 0
is the gravitational constant, and
\sigma>0
is the coefficient of capillary force.

For the two-dimensional fluid, the vorticity
\omega
satisfies the transport equation
(\partial_{t}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\omega=0
,
and it will remain zero if it is zero initially.
One can thus assume the irrotationality condition of the fluid:

\omega=\text{curl }\mathbf{u}=0.

The velocity field
\mathbf{u}
can be expressed as the gradient of some real-valued potential
\phi
:
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\phi
, where the velocity potential
\phi
is a harmonic function that either decays at infinity or is periodic with zero mean.
Therefore, at each time
t
,
\phi
solves the Laplace equation inside
\Omega_{t}
.
By the theory of elliptic PDEs,
\phi(t,x,y)
is determined by its trace on the free boundary
\Gamma_{t}
.
Following the work of Zakharov
[
45
]
, and Craig-Sulem
[
16
]
, let
\eta(t,x)
be the height of the water surface, and
\psi=\psi(t,x)
be the trace of the velocity potential
\phi
on the boundary,

\psi(t,x)=\phi(t,x,\eta(t,x))
.
Then, the water waves can be formulated in terms of a one-dimensional evolution of the pair of unknowns
(\eta,\psi)
; it solves the nonlinear system

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\eta-G(\eta)\psi=0\\
&\partial_{t}\psi+g\eta-\sigma\mathbb{H}(\eta)+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\psi|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\psi+G(\eta)\psi)^{2}}{1+|\nabla\eta|^{2}}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where
G(\eta)
is the Dirichlet-Neumann operator

G(\eta)\psi:=(-\phi_{x}\eta_{x}+\phi_{y})|_{y=\eta(x)}.

This is the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem formulation of the gravity-capillary water waves, which we write it for convenience but never used it throughout the paper.

1.1.
Water waves in holomorphic coordinates

In the rest of this article, instead of the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem formulation, we will use the holomorphic coordinates introduced first by Hunter-Ifrim-Tataru
[
20
]
to study water waves.
The holomorphic coordinates are extensively applied to various water waves models by Ifrim and Tataru, sometimes jointly with collaborators in the following works
[
23
,
22
,
19
,
4
,
5
,
24
]
.
Let
\mathbf{P}
be the projection onto negative frequencies, namely

\mathbf{P}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I}-iH),

with
H
being the Hilbert transform.
Holomorphic functions are defined to be the complex-valued functions whose Fourier transforms are supported on
(-\infty,0]
.
In other words, they satisfy the relation
{\mathbf{P}}f=f
.
Similarly, we define
\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}
to be the projection onto positive frequencies:

\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I}+iH)=\mathbf{I}-{\mathbf{P}}.

Complexed-valued functions such that
\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}f=f
are called anti-holomorphic functions.
Anti-holomorphic functions are complex conjugates of holomorphic functions.
We will write
\alpha
in holomorphic coordinates for the holomorphic spacial variable.

Let
W
be the holomorphic position and
Q
be the holomorphic velocity potential.
The water wave system can be reexpressed in terms of pair of unknowns
(W,Q)
.
The derivation of the gravity-capillary water wave system in holomorphic coordinates can be found in the paper Ifrim-Tataru
[
23
]
and the appendix of Hunter-Ifrim-Tataru
[
20
]
.
The water wave system is given by

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &W_{t}+F(1+W_{\alpha})=0\\
&Q_{t}+FQ_{\alpha}-igW+{\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{|Q_{\alpha}|^{2}}{J}\right]+i\sigma{\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{W_{\alpha\alpha}}{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+W_{\alpha})}-\frac{\bar{W}_{\alpha\alpha}}{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+\bar{W}_{\alpha})}\right]=0,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

where
J:=|1+W_{\alpha}|^{2}
is the Jacobian, and

F={\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{Q_{\alpha}-\bar{Q}_{\alpha}}{J}\right].

The system (
1.1
) has a conserved energy

\mathcal{E}(W,Q)=\Re\int Q\bar{Q}_{\alpha}+2\sigma(J^{\frac{1}{2}}-1-\Re W_{\alpha})+g|W|^{2}(1+W_{\alpha})\,d\alpha.

It also has a conserved horizontal momentum

\mathcal{P}(W,Q)=-i\int Q\bar{W}_{\alpha}-\bar{Q}W_{\alpha}\,d\alpha.

The linearization of (
1.1
) around the zero solution is

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}w_{t}+q_{\alpha}=0&\\
q_{t}-igw+i\sigma w_{\alpha\alpha}=0,&\end{array}\right.

restricted to holomorphic functions.
(
1.2
) can be written as a linear dispersive equation

w_{tt}+igw_{\alpha}-i\sigma w_{\alpha\alpha\alpha}=0.

Its dispersion relation is

\tau^{2}+g\xi+\sigma\xi^{3}=0,\quad\xi\leq 0.

This simplified model suggests that the water waves (
1.1
) is a nonlinear dispersive system.

The conserved energy of (
1.2
) is given by

(1.3)

\mathcal{E}_{0}(w,q)=\int\sigma|w_{\alpha}|^{2}-iq\bar{q}_{\alpha}\,d\alpha=\sigma\|w\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}+\|q\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

The system (
1.2
) is well-posed in
\dot{H}^{1}\times\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}
space.
To study the regularity of nonlinear problem, for appropriate choices of
s
, we will then use the non-homogeneous product spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}
endowed with the norm

\|(w,q)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}:=\|(w,q)\|^{2}_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\times H^{s}},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Going back to the full water wave system (
1.1
), it is a nonlinear degenerate hyperbolic system.
By differentiation, it can be diagonalized and converted into a quasilinear system.
Following the setup in
[
23
]
, we define the differentiated holomorphic unknown functions

({\mathbf{W}},R):=\left(W_{\alpha},\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{1+W_{\alpha}}\right).

The holomorphic function
R
above has an intrinsic meaning; it is the complex velocity restricted on the water surface.
We also need to define three real-valued auxiliary functions.
The first one is the
frequency shift

a
given by

a:=i\left(\bar{\mathbf{P}}\left[\bar{R}R_{\alpha}\right]-{\mathbf{P}}\left[R\bar{R}_{\alpha}\right]\right).

The second one is the
advection velocity

b
given by

b:=2\Re{\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{J}\right].

The third auxiliary function
M
is given by

(1.4)

M:=\frac{R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}+\frac{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}-b_{\alpha}=\bar{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}Y_{\alpha}-R_{\alpha}\bar{Y}]+{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}_{\alpha}-\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y].

Here we use the notation
Y:=\dfrac{W_{\alpha}}{1+W_{\alpha}}
.
Then, using above notations, the pair
({\mathbf{W}},R)
diagonalizes the differentiated
system, and the differentiated water wave system can be written as

(1.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &{\mathbf{W}}_{t}+b{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+\frac{(1+{\mathbf{W}})R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}=(1+{\mathbf{W}})M\\
&R_{t}+bR_{\alpha}-i\frac{g{\mathbf{W}}-a}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}+\frac{i\sigma}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\right]_{\alpha}=\frac{i\sigma}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})}\right]_{\alpha}.\end{aligned}\right.

In the following, we will be mostly working with the differentiated system (
1.5
).

The system (
1.5
) does not have a scaling that keeps both the parameters
g
and
\sigma
unchanged.
The scaling

(1.6)

\left({\mathbf{W}}(t,\alpha),R(t,\alpha)\right)\to\left({\mathbf{W}}(\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}t,\lambda\alpha),\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}R(\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}t,\lambda\alpha)\right)

keeps
\sigma
, but
g
becomes
\frac{g}{\lambda^{2}}
.
This scaling suggests that the critical space of (
1.5
) is
\dot{H}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}\times L^{2}
, and one may hope to prove the well-posedness for (
1.5
) in
\mathcal{H}^{s}
for
s>0
.
In the rest of this article, we will write
\sigma=1
for simplicity.

1.2.
Some historical results on 2D capillary water waves

The literature of the two-dimensional capillary water waves is numerous.
Here we only mention some of the results on the local well-posedness.
Beginning with the works of Nalimov
[
35
]
and Yosihara
[
43
,
44
]
for small smooth data, there are also many results including the work of Agrawal
[
1
,
2
]
, Ambrose-Masmoudi
[
9
]
, Beyer-Gunther
[
13
]
, Coutand-Shkoller
[
15
]
, Iguchi
[
26
]
, Lannes
[
29
]
, Ming-Zhang
[
32
]
and Shatah-Zeng
[
40
]
.

Let us mention the work of Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
6
]
in the local well-posedness.
They worked in Zakharov-Craig-Sulem formulation, and used paradifferential calculus to reduce the water wave system to the following form:

\partial_{t}u+T_{V}\nabla u+iT_{\gamma}u=f.

As a result, they proved that (
1.5
) is locally well-posed in
{\mathcal{H}}^{s}
, for
s>\frac{3}{2}
.

De Poyferré and Nguyen followed the strategy in
[
6
]
and derive a paradifferential reduction for water waves in
[
18
]
.
They combine the energy estimate and the Strichartz estimate to show that (
1.5
) is locally well-posed in
{\mathcal{H}}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, for
s>\frac{27}{20}
in
[
17
]
.

Nguyen in
[
36
]
further used an improved para-composition together with the energy and Strichartz estimates to show the following well-posedness result.

Theorem 1.1
.

The system (
1.5
) is locally well-posed in
{\mathcal{H}}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, for
s>\frac{5}{4}
.

To the author’s knowledge, this is the lowest regularity result before.
See also the works of Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
8
]
, de Poyferré-Nguyen
[
17
]
, Nguyen
[
37
]
and Ai
[
3
]
for Strichartz estimate and its application in the local well-posedness of water waves.

The global well-posedness of two-dimensional gravity-capillary water waves is an open question.
In the pure capillary case, it was shown that the solution is globally well-posed for small localized initial data by Ifrim-Tataru
[
23
]
. Shortly after Ionescu-Pusateri
[
27
]
provided an alternative proof of a similar result.
In the periodic setting, Berti and Delort showed in
[
12
]
for almost all gravity-capillarity parameters, even, small and smooth enough initial data produces almost globally defined in time solutions.
Then Berti-Feola-Franzoi
[
11
]
showed that the periodic gravity-capillary water waves have quadratic lifespans.

The use of holomorphic coordinates for two-dimensional water waves originates in the early work on traveling waves
[
30
]
.
Since then, it has been widely used to study a variety of water wave problems including
[
20
,
22
,
19
,
5
,
4
,
21
,
38
,
39
,
41
]
.
In this article, the main system (
1.5
) was first introduced in
[
23
]
by Ifrim and Tataru.
The authors of
[
23
]
not only show that the solution of pure capillary water waves has a cubic lifespan but also prove that small localized initial data of (
1.1
) leads to global solutions.

One of the main ingredients in the proof of
[
23
]
is the modified energy estimate.
The authors in
[
23
]
used the normal form transformation to construct the modified energy that enables them to establish the modified energy estimate. However the main inspiration in this paper comes from the work of Ai, Ifrim and Tatatru
[
5
]
on pure gravity waves where they developed a new, better class of estimates called
balanced cubic energy estimates
relying on paradifferential form of the equations.
In this article, following
[
23
]
, we will compute the normal form transformation at the paradifferential level for (
1.5
), to obtain the corresponding paradifferential energy estimate for the capillary water wave system.

1.3.
The main results

In this article, we use the paradifferential modified energy estimate to improve the well-posedness result Theorem
1.1
.

To state the energy estimate result, we define the control norm that will be used in the estimates.
Let
\epsilon>0
be an arbitrarily small positive constant.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}_{0}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}+\|R\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}},\quad{\mathcal{A}_{1}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C^{1+\epsilon}_{*}}+\|R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}}.

Here
C^{s}_{*}
is the Zygmund space that will be defined in Section
A
.
For reference, we also define a larger control norm

{\mathcal{A}_{\frac{3}{2}}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\|R\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}.

The subscript
s
of control norm
\mathcal{A}_{s}
represents roughly the difference in terms of derivatives between the
control norm and the scaling.

Theorem 1.2
.

For any
s>1
, there exists an energy functional
E_{s}
which has the following properties:

(1)

Norm equivalence:

(1.7)

E_{s}(\mathbf{W},R)\approx_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}.

(2)

Modified energy estimate:

(1.8)

\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(1+\mathcal{A}_{1}^{2})E_{s}.

For comparison, in
[
18
]
, the energy estimate is the tame energy estimate

\frac{d}{dt}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\left(1+{\mathcal{A}_{\frac{3}{2}}}\right)\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}.

It involves a larger control norm
{\mathcal{A}_{\frac{3}{2}}}
.
As a consequence, a higher regularity well-posedness result is proved in
[
36
]
.

To advance the study of longtime properties of the flow Ifrim and Tataru
[
23
]
proved a better cubic modified energy estimate of the form

\frac{d}{dt}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{0}{\mathcal{A}_{\frac{3}{2}}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}},

also with the
\epsilon
’s removed from the above definition of the control norms
\mathcal{A}_{j}
’s. Following the idea of
balanced cubic energy estimates
of Ai, Ifrim and Tataru
[
5
]
in our estimate (
1.8
) we balance better the two control norms in the last estimate.

For the linearized system, one can also prove a similar linearized modified energy estimate.
This will be discussed in detail in Section
4
.

Having established the energy estimates for the full system and the linearized system, we can get the following local well-posedness result:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
s>1
, the system (
1.5
) is locally well-posed for initial data
({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
in
{\mathcal{H}}^{s}({\mathbb{R}})
(or
\mathbb{T}
) such that
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
is small.
Furthermore, the solution can be continued for as long as
\mathcal{A}_{0}
remains bounded and
(1+\mathcal{A}_{1}^{2})\in L^{1}_{t}
.

Here by local well-posedness, we mean that there exists a positive time
T>0
such that the following properties hold:

(1)

Existence of the solution
: If the initial data is in
\mathcal{H}^{s}
, then there exists a solution
({\mathbf{W}},R)\in\mathcal{H}^{s}
in
[0,T]
, such that

\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{C[0,T;\mathcal{H}^{s}]}\lesssim\|({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})\|_{C[0,T;\mathcal{H}^{s}]}.

(2)

Uniqueness of the solution
: When
s>\frac{3}{2}
, it is unique as is proved in
[
6
]
.
When
\frac{3}{2}\geq s>1
, the solution is unique in the sense that it is the unique limit of regular solutions.

(3)

Continuous dependence on the data for solutions
: If a sequence of initial data
({\mathbf{W}}_{j},R_{j})(0)
converges to
({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
in
\mathcal{H}^{s}
topology, then the solutions
({\mathbf{W}}_{j},R_{j})(t)
also converges to
({\mathbf{W}},R)(t)
in
\mathcal{H}^{s}
, for
t\in[0,T]
.

Our proof of the local well-posedness does not rely on the dispersive properties of the water wave system, such as the Strichartz estimate.
The result holds for both the real line and periodic cases.
In the case of the real line, it achieves a lower
\frac{1}{4}
Sobolev regularity threshold compared to the result in
[
36
]
.
It also obtains a lower
\frac{1}{2}
Sobolev regularity threshold compared to the result
[
6
]
in the periodic case.
For simplicity, we will only discuss the case of the real line in this article.
For the periodic setting, the analysis is essentially the same; we refer the reader to the discussion in Appendix
A
of
[
20
]
for the minor changes.

Compared to the previous result of
[
23
]
, which also uses the modified energy estimate, our result has the following improvements:

•

We express the water waves as a paradifferential system and utilize the paradifferential structure of the equations, akin to the work in
[
5
]
for the case of gravity waves.
Our paradifferential modified energy estimate no longer depends on the larger control norm
{\mathcal{A}_{\frac{3}{2}}}
; it only depends on a smaller control norm
{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.
This effectively lowers the regularity for well-posedness.

•

The normal form analysis is also at the paradifferential level.
As a result, we can allow the Sobolev index
s
to be non-integer, whereas the energy estimates of
[
23
]
only use integer-valued Sobolev index.

•

Our energy estimate for the linearized system Theorem
4.1
is also more balanced with implicit constants depending only on the control norms
\mathcal{A}_{1}
refined, which is better than the energy estimate for the linearized system in
[
23
]
.
We prove a better estimate for the linearized system, and it depends on the control norm
{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.

•

We take into account the effect of gravity and work in the non-homogeneous Sobolev spaces.
The result in
[
23
]
is only on the pure-capillary water waves.

1.4.
The organization of the article

Throughout the analysis of the low regularity well-posedness, paradifferential calculus and paraproducts type estimates for symbols are frequently used.
The full water wave system and the linearized system will also be rewritten in the paradifferential format.
In Appendix
A
, we gather the necessary paradifferential estimates that will be needed in the main part of the article.

In Section
2
, we apply the paraproduct estimates that were developed in
[
5
]
to derive Sobolev and
BMO
estimates for auxiliary functions
a,b,Y
, and so on.
We also compute the leading terms of para-material derivatives of
{\mathbf{W}},R,Y
and
J^{s}
.

Moving on to Section
3
, we prove the paradifferential modified energy estimate Theorem
1.2
.
We write the water waves (
1.5
) as a system of paradifferential equations and compute balanced quadratic paradifferential normal forms to remove the balanced quadratic source terms.
Low-high normal form transformations are used to construct the cubic paradifferential modified energy.
In addition, we also construct the quartic paradifferential modified energy.

After obtaining the energy estimate for the full water wave system, we establish the energy estimate for the linearized water wave system in Section
4
.
We show that the linearized water wave system (
4.1
) is locally well-posed in
\mathcal{H}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}
by writing the linearized system (
4.1
) as a paradifferential system (
4.4
) and constructing the paradifferential modified linearized energy.
The construction approach will be discussed in detail within this section.

Finally, gathering the paradifferential modified energy estimates for both the full and the linearized systems, we outline the proof for the local well-posedness of the water wave system in Section
5
.

Acknowledgments.
The author would like to thank Mihaela Ifrim for introduction and discussion of many important details in her related work
[
5
,
23
,
20
]
.

2.
Water waves related bounds

In the first part of this section, we consider estimates related to the water wave system, following the lead of
[
20
,
5
]
.
We will first give some estimates on the frequency shift
a
and the advection velocity
b
.
Recall that they are given by

a=\Im{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{R}_{\alpha}],\qquad b=2\Re{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R].

Next, we consider the estimates of three auxiliary functions, they are

Y=\frac{{\mathbf{W}}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}},\quad M=2\Re{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}_{\alpha}-\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y],\quad c=2\Im J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}.

Then we give an estimate for the operator
L
, which is a self-adjoint operator defined by

(2.1)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}J^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{\alpha}-ic\partial_{\alpha}-i{\mathbf{P}}c_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\alpha}J^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{\alpha}-i\left(c\partial_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}c_{\alpha}\right)-\frac{i}{2}({\mathbf{P}}c_{\alpha}-\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}c_{\alpha}).

The auxiliary function
Y
is often used as a part of para-coefficients in many of our computations.

c
and
L
will appear in Section
4
when we compute the linearized water waves system.

In the second part of this section, we compute the leading terms of para-material derivatives of
{\mathbf{W}},R,Y
,
J^{s}
, and also the leading term of the commutator
[T_{D_{t}},\mathcal{L}]
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the leading paradifferential part of the operator
L
.

2.1.
Sobolev and Zygmund bounds

We begin with the bounds for the frequency shift
a
, which are similar to those derived in
[
5
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The frequency-shift
a
satisfies the estimate

(2.2)

\|a\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}_{1}^{2},

as well as the Sobolev estimate

(2.3)

\|a\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}},\quad s>0.

Proof.

For the holomorphic part of
a
, we write

{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{R}_{\alpha}]=T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R+{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{R}_{\alpha},R).

Using (
A.3
) and (
A.8
),

\|T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}+\|{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{R}_{\alpha},R)\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}\lesssim\|R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{A}_{1}^{2},

which gives the bound (
2.2
) for the frequency-shift
a
.
For the Sobolev estimate, we use (
A.2
), (
A.4
) to write

\|T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R\|_{H^{s}}+\|{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{R}_{\alpha},R)\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|R\|_{C_{*}^{{\frac{1}{2}}}}\|R\|_{H^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|R\|_{H^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}},

which gives the Sobolev bound (
2.3
) for the frequency-shift
a
.
∎

We continue with the advection velocity
b
, where we again follow
[
5
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The advection velocity
b
satisfies the estimate

(2.4)

\|b\|_{C^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}},

as well as the Sobolev estimate

(2.5)

\|b\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}\|R\|_{H^{s}},\quad s>0.

Proof.

The bounds for
\Re R
are obvious, it suffices to estimate the
{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}]
term.
Note that,

{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}]=T_{\bar{Y}}R+{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R,\bar{Y}).

Using the estimates (
A.3
) and (
A.8
),

\|{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}]\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\leq\|T_{\bar{Y}}R\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}+\|{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R,\bar{Y})\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim\|Y\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|R\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|R\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}},

which gives the
C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}
estimate (
2.4
).
For the Sobolev estimate, we use (
A.5
) and (
A.2
),

\|{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}]\|_{H^{s}}\leq\|T_{\bar{Y}}R\|_{H^{s}}+\|{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R,\bar{Y})\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|Y\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|R\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|R\|_{H^{s}},

which gives the Sobolev bound (
2.5
).
∎

Next, we consider the estimates for the auxiliary function
Y
, also in the spirit of
[
5
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

For
s>0
, the auxiliary function
Y=\frac{{\mathbf{W}}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}
satisfies

(2.6)

\|Y\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s}},\quad\|Y\|_{C^{s}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C^{s}_{*}}.

In particular,
\|Y\|_{C^{1}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.
Moreover, one can write

(2.7)

Y=T_{(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}+E,

where the error
E
satisfies the bound

\|E\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s}}.

The proof of (
2.6
) is a direct consequence of Moser type estimates (
A.11
) and (
A.12
).
The proof of (
2.7
) follows from the paralinearization Lemma
A.7
.

Finally, we derive estimates for auxiliary functions
M
,
c
, and the operator
L
.

Lemma 2.4
.

The auxiliary function
M
satisfies the Zygmund bound

(2.8)

\|M\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

One should compare this with the
L^{\infty}
bound for
M
in
[
5
]
.

Proof.

Recall that
M
can be rewritten as

M=\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{R}Y_{\alpha}-R_{\alpha}\bar{Y}]+{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}_{\alpha}-\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y],

and it suffices to consider the holomorphic part.
We estimate

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}_{\alpha}]\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\leq\|T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}R\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}+\|{\mathbf{P}}(\bar{Y}_{\alpha},R)\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim\|Y_{\alpha}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|R\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y]\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\leq\|T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}Y\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}+\|{\mathbf{P}}(\bar{R}_{\alpha},Y)\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim\|R_{\alpha}\|_{C^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\|Y\|_{C^{1}_{*}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

These give the estimate for
M
(
2.8
).
∎

Recall that the real-valued function
c
is defined by

(2.9)

c:=-\dfrac{i{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}+\dfrac{i\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})}.

Then we have the following estimate for
c
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The auxiliary function
c
satisfies the Zygmund bound

(2.10)

\|c\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}.

Moreover, we have the following representation for
{\mathbf{P}}c
:

(2.11)

{\mathbf{P}}c=-iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+K,

where
K
satisfies the estimate

\|K\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Proof.

By the definition (
2.9
), it suffices to estimate
\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}
.
Its complex conjugate satisfies the similar estimate.
We decompose

\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}=T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1)+\Pi({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1).

Since
J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1
is a function of
Y
,
\bar{Y}
and vanishes when
Y=0
, by the Moser type estimate (
A.12
),

\|J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim\|Y\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}_{0}}.

Hence, we can estimate

\displaystyle\|T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim(1+\|J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}})\|{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim(1+{\mathcal{A}_{0}}){\mathcal{A}_{1}},

\displaystyle\|T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1)\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim\|J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}},

\displaystyle\|\Pi({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1)\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim\|J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}},

and this leads to the Zygmund bound (
2.10
).

To prove the representation for
{\mathbf{P}}c
, we claim that

(2.12)

J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}=T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+K,

Indeed, the factor
J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)
is a smooth function of
{\mathbf{W}}
and
\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}
that equals
1
when
{\mathbf{W}}=0
.
We can apply Moser type of estimate (
A.12
) to conclude that

\displaystyle\|T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)-1\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}+\|\Pi({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)-1)\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}\|J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)-1\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

so that the high-low and the balanced terms can be placed into
K
.
Doing the same analysis for the complex conjugate of
J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}
,

c=-iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}+K.

After applying the projection
{\mathbf{P}}
to eliminate the antiholomorphic term, we get the representation for
{\mathbf{P}}c
(
2.11
).
∎

For the operator
L
, we compute the leading term of
Lw
.

Lemma 2.6
.

The leading term of
Lw
is given by

Lw=T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}w_{\alpha\alpha}-T_{w}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+E,

where the remainder term
E
satisfies

\|E\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|w\|_{H^{1}}.

Proof.

For the first term of
Lw
, we write

\partial_{\alpha}(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}w_{\alpha})=J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}w_{\alpha\alpha}+(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}})_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}=T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}w_{\alpha\alpha}+E.

For the second term of
Lw
, we apply the bound for
c
(
2.10
) to write

\|icw_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|c\|_{L^{\infty}}\|w_{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|w\|_{H^{1}},

so that this term can be put into the remainder term
E
.

As for the third term of
Lw
, by (
2.11
),

-i{\mathbf{P}}c_{\alpha}=-\partial_{\alpha}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+K_{1}=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+K_{1},\quad\|K_{1}\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}_{1}.

Hence, we can write for the last term

-i{\mathbf{P}}c_{\alpha}w=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}w+E=-T_{w}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+E.

Putting these three terms of
Lw
together, we obtain the leading term of
Lw
.
∎

2.2.
Leading terms of para-material derivatives

The material derivative
D_{t}=\partial_{t}+b\partial_{\alpha}
is very important in the water wave system.
At the paradifferential level, it is replaced by the para-material derivative
T_{D_{t}}=\partial_{t}+T_{b}\partial_{\alpha}
, see
[
5
]
.
In the following, we compute the para-material derivatives of various functions. This are similar to the methodology developed in
[
5
]
with the difference that we estimate errors using the control norm
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, as opposed to
\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{2}}
.

We first consider the expressions for para-material derivatives of
W
,
{\mathbf{W}}
and
Y
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
s>0
.

(1)

The unknown
W
satisfies the paradifferential equation

(2.13)

T_{D_{t}}W=-T_{1+W_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R]-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(W_{\alpha},b).

(2)

The leading term of the para-material derivative of
W
is given by

(2.14)

T_{D_{t}}W+T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R=G,

where the source term
G
satisfies the Sobolev bound

\|G\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s-1}},\quad\|G\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

(3)

The unknown
{\mathbf{W}}
satisfies the paradifferential equation

(2.15)

T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}=G_{1},

where the source term
G_{1}
satisfies bounds

\|G_{1}\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}},\quad\|G_{1}\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Proof.

The first equation of (
1.1
) can be rewritten as

W_{t}+b(1+W_{\alpha})=\bar{R},

so that it becomes the paradifferential equation

W_{t}+T_{b}\partial_{\alpha}W=-T_{1+W_{\alpha}}b-\Pi(W_{\alpha},b)+\bar{R}.

After plugging in the expression of
b
and applying the holomorphic projection
{\mathbf{P}}
, we can eliminate the anti-holomorphic portion and obtain the paradifferential equation (
2.13
).

For the leading term of the para-material derivative of
W
, we write

(2.16)

T_{D_{t}}W+T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R=T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},b).

To estimate the right-hand side of (
2.16
), we compute

\displaystyle\|T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|\Pi(\bar{Y},R)\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|Y\|_{C^{1}_{*}}\|R\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|R\|_{H^{s-1}},

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{P}}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},b)\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\|b\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}},

\displaystyle\|T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|\Pi(\bar{Y},R)\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|Y\|_{C^{1}_{*}}\|R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{P}}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},b)\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\lesssim\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C_{*}^{1}}\|b\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Hence, the right-hand side of (
2.16
) can be absorbed into the source term
G
.

Differentiating the equation (
2.14
), one gets

T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}=(T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})}-T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}})R_{\alpha}-T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R+T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}R-T_{b_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+\partial_{\alpha}G.

We use (
A.27
), (
A.4
), and (
2.4
) to estimate the Sobolev bounds of the right-hand side of above equation,

\displaystyle\|(T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}-T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})})R_{\alpha}\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|R\|_{H^{s}},

\displaystyle\|T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R\|_{H^{s}}+\|T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}R\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|R\|_{H^{s}},

\displaystyle\|T_{b_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|b\|_{C^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{*}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{H^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\alpha}G\|_{H^{s}}\leq\|G\|_{H^{s+1}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}.

As for the Zygmund bounds, we use (
A.1
), (
2.4
) and (
A.7
) to write

\displaystyle\|(T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}-T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})})R_{\alpha}\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

\displaystyle\|T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\|T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|R\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

\displaystyle\|T_{b_{\alpha}}W\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\|b_{\alpha}\|_{C^{-1}_{*}}\|W\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\lesssim\|b\|_{C^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{*}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\alpha}G\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\|G\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{3}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Therefore, these terms can be absorbed into the source term
G_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Leading term of the para-material derivative of
Y
is:

T_{D_{t}}Y=-T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}+G_{2}=:G_{3}+G_{2},

where
G_{3}
and
G_{2}
satisfy the Zygmund bounds

\|G_{3}\|_{C^{-\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}},\quad\|G_{2}\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Proof.

Observe that the first equation of (
1.5
) can be expressed using
Y
and
R
as

D_{t}Y+|1-Y|^{2}R_{\alpha}=(1-Y)M.

It can be rewritten as the paradifferential equation

T_{D_{t}}Y+T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}=-T_{R_{\alpha}}(|1-Y|^{2}-1)-\Pi(R_{\alpha},|1-Y|^{2}-1)-T_{Y_{\alpha}}b-\Pi(Y_{\alpha},b)+(1-Y)M.

Using the bounds for the auxiliary functions
Y
(
2.6
),
b
(
2.4
) and
M
(
2.8
), the right-hand side can be bounded by
\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}
in
C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}
space, and these terms can be put into
G_{2}
.
The term
-T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}
can be put into
G_{3}
.
∎

We continue with the para-material derivative of
R
.

Lemma 2.9
.

The leading term of the para-material derivatives is given by

(2.17)

T_{D_{t}}R=-iT_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+K,

where the error
K
satisfies the estimate

\|K\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Proof.

We rewrite the second equation of (
1.5
) as

(2.18)

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}R

\displaystyle=igY-i{\mathbf{P}}[a(1-Y)]-{\mathbf{P}}T_{R_{\alpha}}b-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R_{\alpha},b)

\displaystyle-i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right]+i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right].

For the first and the second terms on the right-hand side of (
2.18
), using (
2.2
) and (
2.6
),

\|gY\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}+\|a\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}+\|aY\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim\|Y\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}+\|a\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}+\|a\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\|Y\|_{C^{\epsilon}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

For the third and the fourth terms on the right-hand side of (
2.18
), we use (
A.7
) and (
A.3
) to shift derivatives:

\|T_{R_{\alpha}}b\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}+\|\Pi(R_{\alpha},b)\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}\lesssim\|R_{\alpha}\|_{C^{\epsilon-{\frac{1}{2}}}_{*}}\|b\|_{C^{{\frac{1}{2}}}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Hence, the first four terms of (
2.18
) can be put into
K
.

As for the two remaining capillary terms, using the relation (
2.12
),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right]={\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+K_{1}\right]=T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+K,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right]={\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-\bar{Y})}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}+K_{1}\right]=K,

where on the first line we use the estimate

\left\|T_{\left(J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)\right)_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right\|_{C_{*}^{\epsilon}}\lesssim\|J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)-1\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{C_{*}^{1+\epsilon}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1},

so that the term
T_{\left(J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)\right)_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}
belongs to
K
.

Finally, we have

(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right]_{\alpha}=(1-Y)T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+K=T_{1-Y}T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+K,

since the balanced and high-low contributions can be put into
K
using estimates (
A.3
) and (
A.7
).
Furthermore, one can combine two paradifferential operators
T_{1-Y}
and
T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}
using paraproduct (
A.27
).

T_{1-Y}T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}=T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+K.

Combining the estimates for each term on the right-hand side of (
2.18
), we obtain the leading term of the para-material derivative of
R
(
2.17
).
∎

Next, we compute the para-material derivative of
J^{s}
for
s\neq 0
.

Lemma 2.10
.

The para-material derivative of
J^{s}
satisfies the relation

(2.19)

T_{D_{t}}J^{s}=-sJ^{s}b_{\alpha}+E_{1},\quad\|E_{1}\|_{C_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

The same expression also holds for the material derivative of
J^{s}
.
One can further write

(2.20)

\partial_{t}J^{s}=-sT_{J^{s}(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}-sT_{J^{s}(1-Y)}\bar{R}_{\alpha}+E_{2},\quad\|E_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}1+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}.

Proof.

Direct computation gives

T_{D_{t}}J^{s}=sJ^{s}(1-Y)T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}+sJ^{s}(1-\bar{Y})T_{D_{t}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}.

For
T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}
, we use the first equation of (
1.5
) to write

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}=

\displaystyle-(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}-T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}b-\Pi({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},b)+(1+{\mathbf{W}})M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+E_{1}.

Here, as in the derivation of (
2.15
),
-T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}b-\Pi({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},b)+(1+{\mathbf{W}})M
can be placed into the error term
E_{1}
.
As a result of the fact
(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-Y)=1
, we have

T_{D_{t}}J^{s}=-sJ^{s}[(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+(1-Y)\bar{R}_{\alpha}]+E_{1}.

Using the identity (
1.4
) and the estimate for
M
(
2.8
),

T_{D_{t}}J^{s}=-sJ^{s}(b_{\alpha}+M)+E_{1}=-sJ^{s}b_{\alpha}+E_{1}.

This gives the para-material derivative of
J^{s}
for
s\neq 0
.
Similarly, for the material derivative of
J^{s}
,

D_{t}J^{s}=sJ^{s}(1-Y)D_{t}{\mathbf{W}}+sJ^{s}(1-\bar{Y})D_{t}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}.

Again applying (
1.5
) and using the same estimates as above, we get that
D_{t}J^{s}
has the same expression as
T_{D_{t}}J^{s}
.

The remainder term
E_{1}
belongs to
E_{2}
, and
T_{b}\partial_{\alpha}J^{s}
can also put into
E_{2}
by Moser type estimate for
J^{s}
(
A.12
):

\|T_{b}\partial_{\alpha}J^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|b\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\|J^{s}-1\|_{C^{1}_{*}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{2}.

We can also write

-sJ^{s}[(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+(1-Y)\bar{R}_{\alpha}]=-sT_{J^{s}(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}-sT_{J^{s}(1-Y)}\bar{R}_{\alpha}+E_{2},

where the high-low and the balanced portion can be put into
E_{2}
.
This gives the proof of (
2.20
) for the time derivative of
J^{s}
.
∎

Finally, we define the operator
\mathcal{L}:=\partial_{\alpha}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}
, which will appear later in the paradifferential linearized system (
4.4
).
We then compute the leading term of the commutator
[T_{D_{t}},\mathcal{L}]
.

Lemma 2.11
.

The leading part of the commutator
[T_{D_{t}},\mathcal{L}]
is given by

(2.21)

[T_{D_{t}},\mathcal{L}]=-{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{\alpha}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}=-\partial_{\alpha}T_{\Re T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{E},

where the operator
\mathcal{E}
satisfies the estimate

\|\mathcal{E}\|_{H^{s+1}\rightarrow H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}_{1}^{2}.

Proof.

We expand the commutator,

\displaystyle[T_{D_{t}},\mathcal{L}]u

\displaystyle=T_{D_{t}}\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}u_{\alpha}\right)-\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}T_{D_{t}}u\right)=\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{T_{D_{t}}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}u_{\alpha}\right)-\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{b_{\alpha}}u_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}u_{\alpha}\right)-\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}u_{\alpha}\right)+\partial_{\alpha}(T_{E}u_{\alpha})+\partial_{\alpha}\left(\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{b_{\alpha}}\right)u_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=-{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{\alpha}\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}u_{\alpha}\right)+\partial_{\alpha}(T_{E}u_{\alpha})+\partial_{\alpha}\left(\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{b_{\alpha}}\right)u_{\alpha}\right),

where
E
is the remainder term in (
2.19
).
The last two terms can be put into the remainder term
\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}u
, since

\displaystyle\|T_{E}u_{\alpha}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|E\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}_{*}}\|u_{\alpha}\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}\|u\|_{H^{s+1}},

\displaystyle\left\|\left(T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{b_{\alpha}}\right)u_{\alpha}\right\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|J^{-\frac{1}{2}}-1\|_{C^{\frac{3}{4}}_{*}}\|b_{\alpha}\|_{C^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\|u_{\alpha}\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1}\|u\|_{H^{s+1}}.

Finally, using the definition of
b
, one can write

\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}b_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=-{\frac{1}{2}}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{b_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{\Re\partial_{\alpha}{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R)]}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{\Re\partial_{\alpha}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R}\partial_{\alpha}+T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{\Re{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{\Re T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}R}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}T_{\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}=-T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=-T_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}-T_{T_{\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}-1}\partial_{\alpha}-T_{\Pi(\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha},J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}-1)}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=-T_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\Re T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}=T_{\Re T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}+\mathcal{E}.

Here at each step, we put the perturbative parts into
\mathcal{E}
.
∎

3.
Paradifferential modified energy estimate for the water waves system

The main goal of this section is to compute the paradifferential normal from transformations for the water waves system (
1.5
) and use them to construct the paradifferential modified energy estimate Theorem
1.2
.

To start the analysis, it is helpful to recast (
1.5
) as a paradifferential system with source terms.
Then, we compute the paradifferential normal form transformations that remove the non-perturbative quadratic terms on the right-hand side of (
3.2
).
These computations are similar to Proposition 3.1 of
[
23
]
, where the normal form transformations for the undifferentiated water wave system (
1.1
) are computed.
Here the normal form transformations for water waves (
1.5
) are calculated at the paradifferential level, and are separated into the balanced and low-high parts.
The balanced quadratic normal form transformations are bounded, whereas low-high quadratic normal form transformations are not.
We will use balanced quadratic normal forms to construct the leading normal form variables and use low-high normal forms to construct the cubic modified energy corrections.
Finally, just as in
[
23
]
, we will construct quartic modified energy corrections to eliminate the remaining non-perturbative quartic energy terms.

In this section, we write
(G,K)
for perturbative source terms that satisfy the bound

(3.1)

\|(G,K)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(1+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{1})\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{s}},\quad s>1.

3.1.
The paradifferential form of the Water Waves

The first result in this section is that the water wave system (
1.5
) can be reformulated as a paradifferential system with given source terms.
These non-perturbative source terms will later be removed by paradifferential normal from transformations.

Lemma 3.1
.

For
s>1
, the water wave system (
1.5
) can be written as

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}{\mathbf{W}}_{t}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R_{\alpha}=\mathcal{G}({\mathbf{W}},R)+G&\\
R_{t}+iT_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}=\mathcal{K}({\mathbf{W}},R)+K,&\end{array}\right.

where
(G,K)
are perturbative source terms that satisfy (
3.1
), and the non-perturbative part of source terms
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}({\mathbf{W}},R)=

\displaystyle-T_{b}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-T_{(1-\bar{Y}){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-(1-\bar{Y})^{2}(1+{\mathbf{W}})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}R-T_{T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}+T_{1-Y}\bar{R}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}

\displaystyle+T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})^{2}}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},R)-{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(T_{1-\bar{Y}}R+T_{1-Y}\bar{R},{\mathbf{W}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}({\mathbf{W}},R)=

\displaystyle 3iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+\frac{5}{2}iT_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}-\frac{i}{2}T_{T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}

\displaystyle+\frac{5}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)+\frac{3}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)-\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha},{\mathbf{W}}\right)-T_{b}R_{\alpha}

\displaystyle-T_{T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}R-T_{T_{1-Y}\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R-\Pi(R_{\alpha},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R)-T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{R},R).

Proof.

We first consider the paradifferential equation for
{\mathbf{W}}
.
According to the para-material derivative of
W
(
2.16
),

W_{t}+T_{b}W_{\alpha}+T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})}R=T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},b)+(T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})}-T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}})R.

Taking the
\alpha
-derivative and using the para-products rule (
A.27
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}}_{t}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R_{\alpha}+T_{b}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+T_{((1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}}))_{\alpha}}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T_{b_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)+T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)-{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},b)+G

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T_{b_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{Y},R)-{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},b)+G

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T_{T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}+T_{1-Y}\bar{R}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})^{2}}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},R)-{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R+T_{1-Y}\bar{R})+G.

Here, we use the definition of
b
and place the perturbative terms in
G
at each step.
This gives the first paradifferential equation of the water waves.

Then we consider the paradifferential equation for
R
.
Recall the equation (
2.18
),

\displaystyle R_{t}+T_{b}R_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=igY-{\mathbf{P}}T_{R_{\alpha}}b-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R_{\alpha},b)-i{\mathbf{P}}[a(1-Y)]

\displaystyle-i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right]+i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right].

For the first three terms on the right-hand side,

igY-{\mathbf{P}}T_{R_{\alpha}}b-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R_{\alpha},b)=-T_{T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}R-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R_{\alpha},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R+T_{1-Y}\bar{R})+K.

For the fourth term on the right-hand side, we use the definition of the auxiliary function
a
,

\displaystyle-i{\mathbf{P}}[a(1-Y)]=-i{\mathbf{P}}T_{1-Y}a+iT_{a}Y+i{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(a,Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T_{1-Y}T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R-T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{R}_{\alpha},R)+K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T_{T_{1-Y}\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{R}_{\alpha},T_{1-Y}R)+K.

As for the first capillary term,

\displaystyle-i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i(1-Y)\partial_{\alpha}T_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1)

\displaystyle-i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1,{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+iT_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}Y+i\Pi\left(Y,T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle-iT_{1-Y}T_{(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y))_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+\frac{3}{2}i(1-Y)\partial_{\alpha}T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}

\displaystyle-iT_{1-Y}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)-1,{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha})+K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+iT_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+i\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}},T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+\frac{3}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}iT_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+\frac{i}{2}T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi\left(3T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right)+K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+\frac{5}{2}iT_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+3iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle+iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha})+\frac{3}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right)+\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right)+K.

Similarly, for the second capillary term,

\displaystyle i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})-1)+i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})-1,\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(1-Y)\partial_{\alpha}T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}-\frac{i}{2}(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi\left(T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}+3T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})^{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right)+K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(1-Y)T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}-\frac{i}{2}(1-Y)T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi\left(T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}+3T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})^{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right)+K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}T_{1-Y}T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}-\frac{i}{2}T_{1-Y}T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha})+K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}T_{T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}-\frac{i}{2}T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha})+K.

Hence, adding two capillary terms, they are

\displaystyle-i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right]+i(1-Y){\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}+3iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+\frac{5}{2}iT_{T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}-\frac{i}{2}T_{T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}

\displaystyle+\frac{5}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)+\frac{3}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)-\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha},{\mathbf{W}}\right)+K.

Putting all the terms together, we obtain the paradifferential system (
3.2
).
∎

3.2.
Computation of normal form transformations

Having rewritten the water wave system as the paradifferential system (
3.2
), we now compute the quadratic normal form transformations
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]},R_{[2]})
that remove the quadratic parts of
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
.
Here and later when we write quadratic terms, the para-coefficients are usually not taken into account, and are simply viewed as constant coefficients.

We consider normal form transformations as the sum of bilinear forms of the following type:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}

\displaystyle=B^{h}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{h}(R,T_{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})^{2}(1-\bar{Y})}R)+B^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{a}(\bar{R},T_{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R)

\displaystyle R_{[2]}

\displaystyle=A^{h}(R,T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{h}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}R)+A^{a}(\bar{R},T_{(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R),

where
A^{h},B^{h},C^{h},D^{h}
are bilinear forms of the holomorphic type, and
A^{a},B^{a},C^{a},D^{a}
are bilinear forms of the mixed type.
For each bilinear form, we can then consider the low-high and the balanced portions.
Direct computation gives,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\partial_{\alpha}R_{[2]}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}A^{h}(R,{\mathbf{W}})-B^{h}(R_{\alpha},{\mathbf{W}})-iC^{h}(R,{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha})-B^{h}({\mathbf{W}},R_{\alpha})-iC^{h}({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha},R)+\partial_{\alpha}D^{h}({\mathbf{W}},R)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\alpha}A^{a}(\bar{R},{\mathbf{W}})-B^{a}(\bar{R}_{\alpha},{\mathbf{W}})-iC^{a}(\bar{R},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha})-B^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},R_{\alpha})+iC^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha},R)+\partial_{\alpha}D^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},R),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}R_{[2]}+iT_{(1-Y)^{2}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-A^{h}(R,R_{\alpha})+i\partial_{\alpha}^{2}C^{h}(R,R)-D^{h}(R_{\alpha},R)-iA^{h}({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha},{\mathbf{W}})+i\partial_{\alpha}^{2}B^{h}({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}})-iD^{h}({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha})

\displaystyle-A^{a}(\bar{R},R_{\alpha})+i\partial_{\alpha}^{2}C^{a}(\bar{R},R)-D^{a}(\bar{R}_{\alpha},R)+iA^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha},{\mathbf{W}})+i\partial_{\alpha}^{2}B^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}})-iD^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}).

We classify the quadratic terms into the holomorphic/ mixed and low-high/ balanced types and compute the normal forms separately.
To eliminate the quadratic source terms, we take the Fourier transform and solve linear systems for symbols.
We write
\mathfrak{a}^{h}(\xi,\eta)
for the symbol of
A^{h}(R,T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})
, and
\mathfrak{a}^{a}(\eta,\zeta)
for the symbol of
A^{a}(\bar{R},T_{(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}})
.
The other symbols are defined similarly.

(i) The low-high holomorphic case:

In this case, we seek low-high holomorphic bilinear forms
A^{h}_{lh},B^{h}_{lh},C^{h}_{lh},D^{h}_{lh}
such that the low-high holomorphic parts of the quadratic normal form transformation
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{h,lh},R_{[2]}^{h,lh})
satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{h,lh}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\partial_{\alpha}R_{[2]}^{h,lh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{R(1-\bar{Y})}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}+T_{(1-\bar{Y}){\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}R+T_{R_{\alpha}(1-\bar{Y})}{\mathbf{W}},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}R_{[2]}^{h,lh}+iT_{(1-Y)^{2}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{h,lh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-\frac{5}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{R(1-\bar{Y})}R_{\alpha}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}}R.

Define the symbol
\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)
that selects the low-high frequencies (
A.16
), the symbols
\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{lh}
,
\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{lh}
,
\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{lh}
,
\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{lh}
of low-high holomorphic bilinear forms
A^{h}_{lh},B^{h}_{lh},C^{h}_{lh},D^{h}_{lh}
then solve the system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\xi+\eta)\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{lh}-\xi\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{lh}+\eta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{lh}=(\xi+\eta)\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)&\\
\eta\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{lh}-\xi^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{lh}-(\xi+\eta)\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{lh}=-\xi\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)&\\
\eta\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{lh}+(\xi+\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{lh}+\xi\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{lh}=-(\xi+\eta)\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)&\\
\xi^{2}\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{lh}-(\xi+\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{lh}+\eta^{2}\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{lh}=3\xi\eta\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)+\frac{5}{2}\xi^{2}\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta).&\end{array}\right.

This system has the solution

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{lh}(\xi,\eta)=\dfrac{(-9\xi^{4}-\xi^{3}\eta+26\xi^{2}\eta^{2}+28\xi\eta^{3}+12\eta^{4})\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)}{2\xi\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{lh}(\xi,\eta)=-\dfrac{(9\xi^{3}+28\xi^{2}\eta+27\xi\eta^{2}+4\eta^{3})\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)}{2\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{lh}(\xi,\eta)=-\dfrac{(3\xi^{3}+6\xi^{2}\eta+11\xi\eta^{2}+6\eta^{3})\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)}{\xi\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{lh}(\xi,\eta)=\dfrac{(6\xi^{3}+15\xi^{2}\eta+7\xi\eta^{2}-4\eta^{3})\chi_{1}(\xi,\eta)}{2\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})}.

(ii) The low-high mixed case:

In this case, we seek low-high mixed bilinear forms
A^{a}_{lh},B^{a}_{lh},C^{a}_{lh},D^{a}_{lh}
such that the low-high mixed parts of the quadratic normal form transformation
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{a,lh},R_{[2]}^{a,lh})
satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{a,lh}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\partial_{\alpha}R_{[2]}^{a,lh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\bar{R}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}-T_{(1-\bar{Y})^{2}(1+{\mathbf{W}})\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha}}R+T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{W}},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}R_{[2]}^{a,lh}+iT_{(1-Y)^{2}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{a,lh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}iT_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{\bar{R}(1-Y)}R_{\alpha}+T_{(1-Y)\bar{R}_{\alpha}}R.

The symbols
\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{lh}
,
\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{lh}
,
\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{lh}
,
\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{lh}
of low-high mixed bilinear forms
A^{a}_{lh},B^{a}_{lh},C^{a}_{lh},D^{a}_{lh}
solve the algebraic system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\zeta-\eta)\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{lh}+\eta\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{lh}+\zeta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{lh}=(\zeta-\eta)\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)&\\
\zeta\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{lh}+\eta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{lh}-(\zeta-\eta)\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{lh}=-\eta\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)&\\
\zeta\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{lh}+(\zeta-\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{lh}-\eta\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{lh}=-(\zeta-\eta)\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)&\\
\eta^{2}\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{lh}+(\zeta-\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{lh}-\zeta^{2}\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{lh}=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta).&\end{array}\right.

The solution of this algebraic system is given by

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{lh}(\eta,\zeta)=-\dfrac{(6\eta^{4}-15\eta^{3}\zeta+20\eta^{2}\zeta^{2}-20\eta\zeta^{3}+12\zeta^{4})\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)}{2\eta(\zeta-\eta)(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{lh}(\eta,\zeta)=\dfrac{(2\eta^{3}-3\eta^{2}\zeta+7\eta\zeta^{2}-14\zeta^{3})\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)}{2(\zeta-\eta)(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{lh}(\eta,\zeta)=\dfrac{\zeta(5\eta^{2}-7\eta\zeta+6\zeta^{2})\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)}{\eta(\zeta-\eta)(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{lh}(\eta,\zeta)=\dfrac{(8\eta^{3}-20\eta^{2}\zeta+23\eta\zeta^{2}-14\zeta^{3})\chi_{1}(\eta,\zeta)}{2(\zeta-\eta)(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})}.

(iii) The balanced holomorphic case:

In this case, we seek balanced holomorphic bilinear forms
A^{h}_{hh},B^{h}_{hh},C^{h}_{hh}
such that the balanced holomorphic parts of the quadratic normal form transformation
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{h,hh},R_{[2]}^{h,hh})
satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{h,hh}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\partial_{\alpha}R_{[2]}^{h,hh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(T_{1-\bar{Y}}R,{\mathbf{W}}),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}R_{[2]}^{h,hh}+iT_{(1-Y)^{2}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{h,hh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi(R_{\alpha},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R)-\frac{5}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)-\frac{3}{2}iT_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-Y)^{3}}\Pi\left({\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}\right).

Define the symbol
\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)
that selects the balanced frequencies (
A.17
) and
m_{sym}
be the symmetrization of the bilinear symbol
m
, the symbols
\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh}
,
\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}
,
\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}
of bilinear forms
A^{h}_{hh},B^{h}_{hh},C^{h}_{hh}
then solve the system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\xi+\eta)\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh}-2\xi\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}+2\eta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}=(\xi+\eta)\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)&\\
(\eta\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh})_{sym}+(\xi+\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}=-\frac{1}{2}(\xi+\eta)\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)&\\
(\xi^{2}\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh})_{sym}-(\xi+\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}=\frac{1}{4}(5\xi^{2}+6\xi\eta+5\eta^{2})\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta).&\end{array}\right.

From the first equation of the system, we get

\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh}=2\frac{\xi}{\xi+\eta}\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}-2\frac{\eta^{2}}{\xi+\eta}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}+\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta).

Then we get two symmetrized symbol

\displaystyle(\eta\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh})_{sym}=2\frac{\xi\eta}{\xi+\eta}\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}-\frac{\xi^{3}+\eta^{3}}{\xi+\eta}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}+\frac{\xi+\eta}{2}\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta),

\displaystyle(\xi^{2}\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh})_{sym}=\frac{\xi^{3}+\eta^{3}}{\xi+\eta}\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}-2\frac{\xi^{2}\eta^{2}}{\xi+\eta}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}+\frac{\xi^{2}+\eta^{2}}{2}\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta).

Substituting these into the second and the third equation of the system,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}2\xi\eta\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}+3(\xi+\eta)\xi\eta\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}=-(\xi+\eta)^{2}\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)&\\
3(\xi+\eta)\xi\eta\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}+2\xi^{2}\eta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}=-\frac{3}{4}(\xi+\eta)^{3}\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta).&\end{array}\right.

The solution is then given by

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{h}_{hh}(\xi,\eta)=-\dfrac{(27\xi^{4}+39\xi^{3}\eta+23\xi^{2}\eta^{2}-3\xi\eta^{3}-6\eta^{4})\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)}{2\xi\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{hh}(\xi,\eta)=-\dfrac{3(\xi+\eta)^{2}(9\xi^{2}+10\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)}{4\xi\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{h}_{hh}(\xi,\eta)=-\dfrac{3(\xi+\eta)^{3}\chi_{2}(\xi,\eta)}{2\xi\eta(9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2})}.

(iv) The balanced mixed case:

In this case, we seek balanced mixed bilinear forms
A^{a}_{hh},B^{a}_{hh},C^{a}_{hh},D^{a}_{hh}
such that the balanced mixed parts of the quadratic normal form transformation
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{a,hh},R_{[2]}^{a,hh})
satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{a,hh}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\partial_{\alpha}R_{[2]}^{a,hh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(T_{1-Y}\bar{R},{\mathbf{W}})-T_{(1+{\mathbf{W}})(1-\bar{Y})^{2}}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},R),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}R_{[2]}^{a,hh}+iT_{(1-Y)^{2}J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{a,hh}+\text{cubic and higher terms}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi(\bar{R},R)-\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha\alpha}\right)+\frac{i}{2}T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-Y)}{\mathbf{P}}\Pi\left(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha},{\mathbf{W}}\right).

Then for symbols
\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{hh}
,
\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{hh}
,
\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{hh}
,
\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{hh}
of bilinear forms
A^{a}_{hh},B^{a}_{hh},C^{a}_{hh},D^{a}_{hh}
, we have the algebraic system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\zeta-\eta)\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{hh}+\eta\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{hh}+\zeta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{hh}=(\zeta-\eta)\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}&\\
\zeta\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{hh}+\eta^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{hh}-(\zeta-\eta)\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{hh}=(\zeta-\eta)\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}&\\
\zeta\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{hh}+(\zeta-\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{hh}-\eta\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{hh}=-(\zeta-\eta)\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}&\\
\eta^{2}\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{hh}+(\zeta-\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{hh}-\zeta^{2}\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{hh}=\frac{1}{2}(\eta^{2}-\zeta^{2})\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}.&\end{array}\right.

Here the indicator function
1_{\zeta<\eta}
is the symbol for the frequency projection
{\mathbf{P}}
.
Then the solution of this algebraic system is

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{a}_{hh}(\eta,\zeta)=\dfrac{3(\eta-\zeta)(2\eta^{2}-\eta\zeta+2\zeta^{2})\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}}{2\eta(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}^{a}_{hh}(\eta,\zeta)=-\dfrac{(\eta-\zeta)(2\eta^{2}+\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}}{2\eta(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{a}_{hh}(\eta,\zeta)=-\dfrac{3\zeta\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}}{\eta(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{a}_{hh}(\eta,\zeta)=-\dfrac{(\eta-\zeta)(8\eta^{2}-8\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})\chi_{2}(\eta,\zeta)1_{\zeta<\eta}}{2\eta(4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2})}.

To conclude, we have shown the following result:

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that
({\mathbf{W}},R)
solve the water wave system (
3.2
).
There exists normal form transformations

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}

\displaystyle=B^{h}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{h}(R,T_{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})^{2}(1-\bar{Y})}R)+B^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{a}(\bar{R},T_{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R),

\displaystyle R_{[2]}

\displaystyle=A^{h}(R,T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{h}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}R)+A^{a}(\bar{R},T_{(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{a}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R),

such that the modified unknowns
(\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}},\tilde{R}):=({\mathbf{W}}+{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]},R+R_{[2]})
solve the system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{t}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}\tilde{R}_{\alpha}=\tilde{\mathcal{G}}({\mathbf{W}},R)+G&\\
\tilde{R}_{t}+iT_{J^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-Y)^{2}}\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}=\tilde{\mathcal{K}}({\mathbf{W}},R)+K,&\end{array}\right.

where the leading parts of the source terms
(\tilde{\mathcal{G}},\tilde{\mathcal{K}})
do not contain quadratic terms.
The holomorphic bilinear forms
A^{h},B^{h},C^{h},D^{h}
and mixed bilinear forms
A^{a},B^{a},C^{a},D^{a}
can be further separated into the low-high and balanced portions.
Their symbols are given in (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) respectively.

Taking a close look at the normal form transformations
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]},R_{[2]})
, one may hope that they are bounded in the sense that

(3.7)

\|({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]},R_{[2]})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}},\quad s>1.

One can check that the balanced parts of the normal form transformations

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{hh}

\displaystyle=B^{h}_{hh}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{h}_{hh}(R,T_{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})^{2}(1-\bar{Y})}R)+B^{a}_{hh}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{a}_{hh}(\bar{R},T_{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R),

\displaystyle R_{[2]}^{hh}

\displaystyle=A^{h}_{hh}(R,T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+A^{a}_{hh}(\bar{R},T_{(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{a}_{hh}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R),

satisfy the bound (
3.7
), where the symbols are given in (
3.5
) and (
3.6
).
The balanced parts of the normal form transformations
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{hh},R_{[2]}^{hh})
eliminate the balanced parts of the cubic source terms
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
in (
3.2
).
Plugging
({\mathbf{W}}+{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{hh},R+R_{[2]}^{hh})
into (
3.2
) also produces cubic terms, which are perturbative and satisfy (
3.1
).

As for the low-high parts of the normal form transformations
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh},R_{[2]}^{lh})
, they are

\displaystyle{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh}

\displaystyle=B^{h}_{lh}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{h}_{lh}(R,T_{J^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})^{2}(1-\bar{Y})}R)+B^{a}_{lh}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}{\mathbf{W}})+C^{a}_{lh}(\bar{R},T_{J^{{\frac{1}{2}}}(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R),

\displaystyle R_{[2]}^{lh}

\displaystyle=A^{h}_{lh}(R,T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{h}_{lh}({\mathbf{W}},T_{1-Y}R)+A^{a}_{lh}(\bar{R},T_{(1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}})+D^{a}_{lh}(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},T_{1-\bar{Y}}R),

whose symbols are given in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
).
Unfortunately,
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh},R_{[2]}^{lh})
do not satisfy the bound (
3.7
).
One cannot use
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh},R_{[2]}^{lh})
directly to eliminate the low-high parts of the cubic source terms
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
in (
3.2
).

3.3.
Construction of almost balanced cubic modified energy

Having computed the quadratic paradifferential normal form transformations, we are now in a position to mimic the
balanced cubic energy method
of Ai, Ifrim and Tataru first developed in
[
5
]
, and construct the corresponding almost balanced cubic paradifferential modified energy for our model problem.

To achieve the norm equivalence property (
1.7
), our first idea is to consider the energy

E^{1}_{s}=\int T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}+|\langle D\rangle^{s}R|^{2}\,d\alpha.

This choice of energy, however, violates the modified energy estimate (
1.8
).
Indeed, we compute the time derivative of
E_{s}^{1}
using (
3.2
),

(3.8)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{1}_{s}=\int T_{\partial_{t}J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mathbf{W}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle-2\Re\int T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}T_{(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})}R_{\alpha}-i\langle D\rangle^{s}R\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s}T_{J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})^{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{\alpha\alpha}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle-2\Re\int\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}G+\langle D\rangle^{s}R\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s}\bar{K}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle-2\Re\int\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{G}+\langle D\rangle^{s}R\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s}\bar{\mathcal{K}}\,d\alpha.

The first integral on the right-hand side of (
3.8
) is non-perturbative due to (
2.20
), we will later construct a cubic energy correction to eliminate it.
For the second integral of (
3.8
), we add the para-coefficient
T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}
to get
J^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-\bar{Y})(1+{\mathbf{W}})=J^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}(1-\bar{Y})^{2}
, so that the leading part of this integral cancels using integration by parts.
The third integral of
\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}^{1}
is perturbative, whereas the last integral of (
3.8
) is not, since source terms
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
do not satisfy the bound (
3.1
).

To eliminate the last non-perturbative integral of
\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}^{1}
, we then choose the modified unknowns
(\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}},\tilde{R})=({\mathbf{W}}+{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]},R+R_{[2]})
, and consider the modified energy

\tilde{E}_{s}=\int T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\bar{\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}}+|\langle D\rangle^{s}\tilde{R}|^{2}\,d\alpha.

This new choice of energy, on the other hand, does not seem to satisfy the norm equivalence property (
1.7
), because low-high quadratic normal forms
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh},R_{[2]}^{lh})
do not satisfy the bound (
3.7
).
It also produces more quartic and higher integral terms that are not perturbative.
In the following, we will modify this energy
\tilde{E}_{s}
and construct the desired modified energy that satisfies both conditions.

As discussed in the previous subsection, the balanced part of the modified unknowns
(\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}^{hh},\tilde{R}^{hh})=({\mathbf{W}}+{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{hh},R+R_{[2]}^{hh})
remove the balanced part of the non-perturbative source terms of
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
in (
3.2
), and
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{hh},R_{[2]}^{hh})
are bounded by (
3.7
).
Hence, we can choose

\tilde{E}_{s}^{1}=\int T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}^{hh}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\bar{\tilde{{\mathbf{W}}}}^{hh}+|\langle D\rangle^{s}\tilde{R}^{hh}|^{2}\,d\alpha.

Then we consider the unbalanced cubic part of
\tilde{E}_{s}
, which is given by

\displaystyle\int T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}{\mathbf{W}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}_{[2]}^{lh}+T_{J^{-\frac{3}{2}}}\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}{\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh}\,d\alpha

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int\langle D\rangle^{s}R\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s}\bar{R}_{[2]}^{lh}+\langle D\rangle^{s}\bar{R}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{s}R_{[2]}^{lh}\,d\alpha.

Plugging in the symbols of
({\mathbf{W}}_{[2]}^{lh},R_{[2]}^{lh})
computed in Proposition
3.2
and applying the Plancherel theorem, we now successively compute each part of this cubic integral.
Note that when
\zeta=\xi+\eta
,

4\eta^{2}-4\eta\zeta+9\zeta^{2}=9\xi^{2}+14\xi\eta+9\eta^{2}.

In the following, when we write for example
T_{\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}(\xi)}\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}(\eta)
, we abuse the notation for low-high para-products, and this means the frequencies
|\xi|\ll|\eta|
.

(1)

The cubic integrals that only involve
\widehat{{\mathbf{W}}}
.
This part of integrals is given by

\displaystyle I_{1}^{s}=
